D. 476), the founder of<br>Indian astronomy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a>; maintained the<br>rotation of the earth on its axis and<br>explained eclipses of sun and moon,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Aryans. <em>See under</em> Hinduism <em>and</em> Vedic<br>Literature.</p><p class="index-entry">Aryas or 'kinsmen,' the name given by<br>themselves to the conquering invaders,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Asoka the Great, king of Magadha or<br>Behār (272–231 B.C.), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>; his<br>eight years' enjoyment of the chase and<br>the table, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>; his murderous conquest<br>(261) of Kalinga, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>; his conversion<br>by pity and remorse for bloodshed to<br>the teaching and practice of Buddhism,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>; became a lay and finally an ordained member, 24 (<em>n.</em>), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>; his<br>ardent and successful propaganda by<br>royal edicts, doctrinal inscriptions,<br>missions, and the foundation of monasteries, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-86">44</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-326">284</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>; his care for the<br>bodies as well as the souls of his subjects and proselytes by mild administration, good roads, hospitals, &amp;c., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>;<br>transformed a local sect into a world-religion, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>; his Buddhism the present<br>religion of Ceylon, Burma, and Siam,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>; his active toleration and concurrent<br>endowment of other religions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-200">158</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>;<br>the disintegration of the empire after<br>his death, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-327">285</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Asoka, some references to: his date misplaced by 900 years in epigraphy and<br>the Purānas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-58">16</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-64">22</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-65">23</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>; his true<br>date determined (1793) by Sir Wm.<br>Jones, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>; his pillars and edicts, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-95">53</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-148">106</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-151">109</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-200">158</a>; his <em>stūpas</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-153">111</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-199">157</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-200">158</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-201">159</a>; no known coins of,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-180">138</a>; his columns, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-77">35</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-78">36</a>; his rock<br>edicts, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-83">41</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-95">53</a>; the historical value<br>of his inscriptions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-95">53</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-96">54</a>; his abdication and religious retirement, 24 (<em>n.</em>),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-96">54</a>; his visit and honour to Buddha's birthplace before his conversion,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-96">54</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-97">55</a>; the history of Indian art begins<br>with, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-145">103</a>; pulled down and rebuilt the<br>first <em>stūpas</em> over Buddha's remains, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-201">159</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-576"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="576" data-folio="534" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n575_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 534">[p. 534]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Assam, king of, tributary to Harsha of Kanauj, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-341">299</a> ; ceded (1826) by Treaty of Yandabu, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-539">497</a> ; coinage of Ahom dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Assamese literature, chiefly historical, but also poetical and medical, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-476">434</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Astronomy, information on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-107">65</a> ; Indian progress in, due to Greek science, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a> ; the great Indian astronomers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-383">341</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Athaide, Luis de, Portuguese Viceroy (1568–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-113">71</a>, 1578–81), defended Goa and other settlements against the Sultān of Bijāpur and the combined Muhammadan forces, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a> ; thrice repulsed the Achīnese from Malacca, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-493">451</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Atharvaveda, a later (c. 600 <span class="sc">b.c.</span>) and originally a non-canonical Veda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a> ; a collection mainly of spells, sorcery, and domestic rites, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a> ; an interesting and valuable manual of popular superstition, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a> ; recognized as the fourth Veda in the second century <span class="sc">b.c.</span>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Auckland, Lord, Governor-General (1836–42), began a new era of war and conquest for twenty years, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-541">499</a> ; his ill-fated policy of military interference in Afghānistān (1837–42), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-541">499</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-542">500</a> ; the massacre at Kābul (1841) and of the</p><p class="index-entry">Lodī (1526) at Pānīpat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>, and the Hindu Rājās under Sanga Singh (1527) at Kanwa, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a> ; his speedy reduction of the Delhi kingdom and Jaunpur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Babylonian influence on Indian art, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-145">103</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a> ; on Indian coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bactrian, or Graeco-Bactrian, coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bādāmi, capital of Western Chālukyas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-369">327</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bahādur Shāh, king (1526–37) of Gujarāt, annexed (1531) Mālwā, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-419">377</a> ; supported the Lodīs against Humāyūn and was expelled from his kingdom, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-419">377</a> ; killed on board a Portuguese ship off Diu in the course of negotiations, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-419">377</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bahādur Shāh, Mughal emperor (1707–12), defeated his brothers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; expeditions against Rājputs and Sikhs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-447">405</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bahlol Lodī, son of a horse-dealer, founder of the Lodī dynasty (1451–89), kings of Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-409">367</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bahmanīs of Gulbarga and Bidār, Muhammadan dynasty of Southern India (1347–1526), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-425">383</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-426">384</a> ; their extensive rule from sea to sea, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-425">383</a> ; their wars with Vijayanagar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-386">344</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-388">346</a>, Mālwā and Gujarāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-425">383</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-426">384</a> ; their later decline due to partition among revolted garrison in the passes, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-542">500</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-543">501</a> ; Dr.<br>Brydon the sole survivor, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-544">502</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Aurangzeb (Ālamgīr I), sixth Mughal<br>emperor of India, third son and successor of Jahāngīr (1658–1707), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a> ;<br>deposed his father, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a> ; murdered his<br>brothers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-444">402</a> ; his constant wars, 401–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; the rise of the Marāthās under<br>Sivajī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-444">402</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a> ; the ill success of<br>the imperial forces, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-444">402</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a> ; revolt of<br>his son at the head of the Rājputs,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a> ; his campaign of twenty-five years<br>(1682–1707) in the Deccan against the<br>Muhammadan kingdoms and Marāthās,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; his successful subjugation of<br>the whole of India as far as Tanjore,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; the Mughal empire at its height,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; the reasons of its speedy decline<br>henceforth, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; estimate of his character<br>and policy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a> ; his bigotry and persecution of Hindus, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a> ; the<br>return to orthodoxy in his coinage,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-189">147</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-190">148</a> ; the decline of art under, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Avantivarman (A. D. 855–83), famous<br>king of Kashmīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-352">310</a> ; his two great<br>temples at Avantipura and irrigation<br>works, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-352">310</a>.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">B.</h3><p class="index-entry">Bābar (1483–1530), fifth in descent from<br>Taimūr, a Barlās Turk, first Mughal<br>emperor of Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a> ; his small<br>beginnings, early adventures, and capture of Kabūl, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a> ; defeated Ibrāhīm generals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-425">383</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-426">384</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-427">385</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-430">388</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-433">391</a> ;<br>a table of the dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-427">385</a> ; their<br>mosques and tombs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-235">193</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-237">195</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bairām Khān, Humāyūn’s general and<br>Akbar’s tutor (1554–60), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-439">397</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a> ;<br>defeated Hīmū at Pānīpat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-439">397</a> ; resented and resisted his pupil’s assumption of government, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a> ; submitted<br>and was pardoned, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bājī Rao II, seventh and last Peshwā<br>(1795–1818), his disputed supremacy,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a> ; flight to British protection, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a> ;<br>accepted (1802) Treaty of Bassein, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a> ;<br>reinstated after third Marāthā War,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a> ; intrigues with chiefs against<br>British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a> ; attack on the Resident<br>at Poona, defeat in fourth War, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a> ;<br>deposition and confiscation of his dominions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a> ; died (1853) a pensioner,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a> ; Nāna Sāhib his adopted son, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bālajī Vishvanāth, minister of Shāhu and<br>first Peshwā (1718–20), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a> ; organizer<br>of the Marāthā confederacy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a> ; extorted from the emperor <em>chauth</em> for the<br>Deccan and recognition of Shāhu’s<br>kingdom, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-448">406</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Balban (Ulugh Khān), last Slave king but<br>one of Delhi (1266–86), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-401">359</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-403">361</a> ; originally the brother and father-in-law<br>and Wazīr of Nāzīr-ud-dīn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-401">359</a> ; his<br>consolidation of the empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-402">360</a> ;<br>operations against Rājputs and Mongols, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-402">360</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-403">361</a> ; a strong, severe ruler,<br>and patron of literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-403">361</a> ; he and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-577" data-page="577" data-folio="535" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n576_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 535">[p. 535]</button> his descendants in Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-413">371</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-414">372</a>;<br>his coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-186">144</a>.<br>Bankim Chandra (1838-94), a Bengali<br>novelist and magazine editor, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-475">433</a>.<br>Bantam, in Java, factory (1601) of East<br>India Company at, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-497">455</a>; its early importance, political and commercial,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-497">455</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-500">458</a>; made (1630) subordinate to<br>Surat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-499">457</a>; its fall (1682) and withdrawal of British from Java, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-499">457</a>.<br>Barābar caves, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-153">111</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-203">161</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-204">162</a>.<br>Barīd Shāhīs of Bīdar, Muhammadan<br>dynasty of (1492-1609), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-433">391</a>; founded<br>by a revolter from the Bahmanīs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-433">391</a>;<br>a table of the dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-433">391</a>.<br>Barlow, Sir George, Governor-General<br>(1805), abandoned the Rājputs to Holkar and Sindhia, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>.<br>Baroda, Marāthā Gaikwār of, ever the<br>friend of the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-537">495</a>;<br>chief deposed (1875) and replaced by<br>another member of his family, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a>.<br>Bassein, Treaty of, concluded (1802) between the Peshwā and Wellesley, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>; its surrender of Marāthā independence resented by the chiefs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>.<br>Bengal, under Samudragupta (A. D. 350),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-333">291</a>; under Harsha (606), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-341">299</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-342">300</a>; its<br>four mediaeval kingdoms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-358">316</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>;<br>Hinduized by the Sen dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>;<br>under Muhammadan rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-230">188</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-401">359</a>;<br>under independent Muhammadan kings<br>(1202-1576), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-413">371</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-415">373</a>; under the Mughal and Sur dynasties, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>; its<br>native rulers or Nawābs (1707-56),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; the removal of its capital from<br>Dacca to Murshidābād, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; Sirāj-ud-daula, Nawāb (1756-7), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>;<br>capture of Calcutta, the Black Hole<br>(1756), and battle of Plassey, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>;<br>Mīr Jafar (1757-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-102">60</a>, 1763-5), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-51">9</a>;<br>Clive's first Governorship of Bengal<br>(1758-60), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>; his second (1765-7),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; grant of the Dīwānī (1765),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; Warren Hastings, Governor<br>(1772-4), first Governor-General (1774-<br>85), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-523">481</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-526">484</a>; expressed in terms of Latin<br>Christianity, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-456">414</a>; the inspirer of its<br>vernacular literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-456">414</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-458">416</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-467">425</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-468">426</a>;<br>as a means of salvation opposed to<br>knowledge, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-296">254</a>.<br>Bhandarkar, Dr., quoted, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-378">336</a> <em>et passim</em>.<br>Bharatas, one of the Vedic tribes in the<br>Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-264">222</a>; History of<br>British Rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-572">530</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bidār, capital after 1428 of the Bahmanī<br>kings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-235">193</a>; its mosque and other<br>architectural remains, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-236">194</a>; the Barīd<br>Shāhīs of (1492-1609), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-433">391</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bidpai, Fables of. <em>See Pañchatantra.</em></p><p class="index-entry">Bihār. <em>See Magadha.</em></p><p class="index-entry">Bihārī Lāl, a Western Hindī lyric (Kṛishṇa) poet of Jaipur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-465">423</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bihārī literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-474">432</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bijāpur, kingdom and capital of the ‘Ādil<br>Shāhī (Turk) dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-238">196</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>, 385–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-429">387</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-442">400</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; the influence of (Persian)<br>Shiahism upon its architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>; its<br>principal architectural buildings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>; their conversion into offices and<br>official residences by the Bombay Government, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>. <em>See also Ādil Shāhīs.</em></p><p class="index-entry">Bilhana, a poet of Kashmīr (c. A.D. 1050),<br>condemned to death for an intrigue with<br>a princess, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-284">242</a>; won his life and wife<br>by his ‘Fifty Stanzas of the Thief,’ 242;<br>his imaginative chronicle of Vikramāditya VI, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-379">337</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bimbisāra, unfortunate Magadhan king,<br>friend and patron of Gautama Buddha,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-315">273</a>; his conquests and forces, as reported to Alexander, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bindusāra (B.C. 297–272), son of Chandragupta, and second Mauryan emperor,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>; his relations with Seleucus,<br>and possibly with Ptolemy Philadelphus of Egypt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-325">283</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Böhtlingk’s <em>Indische Sprüche</em>, a treasury<br>and translation of the best stanzas of<br>Sanskrit literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-294">252</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bombay, acquisition of (1688), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-501">459</a>;<br>made seat of Western Presidency from<br>Surat (1684–7), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-501">459</a>; troubled by the<br>Marāthās, sea and land, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-504">462</a>; the<br>aggressive policy (1775) of its Government towards the Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>;<br>the Treaty of Surat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-527">485</a>; its ignominious failure to nominate a Peshwā,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-527">485</a>; its rescue by Hastings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-527">485</a>; the brilliant campaign (1778-9)<br>of Goddard, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-527">485</a>; constituted as<br>it is after last Marāthā War, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bone implements rare in India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-133">91</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bower Manuscript, the, an original document of A.D. 400–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>, from Kashgar,<br>written on birch-bark, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-52">10</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Braganza, Constantino de, Portuguese<br>Viceroy (1558-61), took Damān, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Brāhman, the development of the significance of the term, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-273">231</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-296">254</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Brāhmaṇas, the (800–500 B. C.), ritual<br>and speculative textbooks of sacrifice,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-251">209</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-272">230</a>; the <em>Śatapatha</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-272">230</a>;<br>the Upanishads, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-272">230</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-274">232</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Brāhmans and Brāhmanism, in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>; in the later Vedas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-269">227</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>; in the Mahābhārata, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-277">235</a>; ridiculed<br>in the <em>Pañchatantra</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-293">251</a>; its philosophical systems, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>; its freedom<br>of thought, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>; in Neo-Hinduism,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-347">305</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-350">308</a>; in Rājput civilization, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-357">315</a>.<br><em>See also under</em> Religions, Sacrifice.</p><p class="index-entry">Brass, numerous inscriptions on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-67">25</a>; the<br>most important, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-67">25</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Brick, architecture in, a necessity in Bengal<br>due to the absence of stone, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-230">188</a>; the characteristics of the Bengal style, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-230">188</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-231">189</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Brick votive tablets, mostly Buddhist,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-79">37</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-82">40</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Bṛihat-kathā</em>, a collection of stories in<br>Prākrit of the first or second century,<br>known from Sanskrit translations, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">British Rule in India (1707–1905), chap.<br>xiv, pp. <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-512">470</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-572">530</a>; to the battle of Wandiwāsh and the fall of French power<br>(1760), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-512">470</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; Clive (1751–67),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; Lord Wellesley (1798–<br>1805) and the policy of British supremacy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-530">488</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>; seal-stamps,<br>seals attached to copperplate records,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>, and a signet-ring, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-73">31</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bronze Age, the, elsewhere between Stone<br>(Neolithic) and Iron Ages, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>; his later<br>colossal and other statues, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-89">47</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-90">48</a>; the<br>place of his birth, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-96">54</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-97">55</a>, of his<br>early life, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-86">44</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-97">55</a>; his royal friend and<br>patron, Bimbisāra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-315">273</a>; died probably<br>in 487 <span class="sc">B. C.</span>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-316">274</a>, or in 482 <span class="sc">B. C.</span>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-96">54</a>;<br>his relics at Piprahwa, <a class="sc">B. C.</span> to <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 350, but never universal<br>nor even general, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-340">298</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>; its decay due to natural causes<br>and not to persecution, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-163">121</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-340">298</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-341">299</a>;<br>its rapid decline and fall in the mediaeval period, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-157">115</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>; the old and<br>new contrasted, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-156">114</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>, rivalry<br>between, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>; the Messianic and Western character of the theology and art<br>of the new, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-156">114</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-157">115</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-207">165</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-209">167</a>; in epigraphy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-43">1</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-130">88</a> <em>passim</em>; its intimate<br>association with ancient Indian art,<br>sculpture, and architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-146">104</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-164">122</a><br><em>passim</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-212">170</a> <em>passim</em>; its philosophical basis on the Sānkya system, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>;<br>its points of contact and contrast with<br>Jainism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>; the language of its<br>old sacred canon of the South, Pāli,<br>an old Prākrit, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-250">208</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>; ‘The Three<br>Baskets’ and a vast literature subsidiary<br>to the canon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>; the fragments left of<br>the Sanskrit canon of the North, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>; its Chinese and Tibetan translations, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>; its legendary ‘Life of<br>Buddha,’ 260, and other works of edification, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>; now unknown in India<br>proper, but a world-religion in China,<br>Tibet, Burma, and Japan, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bühler, Professor, his <em>Indische Palaeographie</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-44">2</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-115">73</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-364">322</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-366">324</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bukka, one of the two brothers founders<br>(1336–79) of the Vijayanagar empire,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-386">344</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-387">345</a>; his celebrated minister,<br>Mādhavāchārya, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-386">344</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Bull and Horseman’ coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-184">142</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-185">143</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-186">144</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bundelās, Rājput ruling race in Bundelkhand, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-360">318</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Burma, progress of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-566">524</a>; made (1897) a<br>Lieutenant-Governorship, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-568">526</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Burmese literature, mainly historical and<br>dramatic, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-479">437</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-480">438</a>; its religious writings in Pāli, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-479">437</a>; Shan literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-480">438</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Burmese Wars, first (1824–6), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-538">496</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-539">497</a>; the Treaty of Yandabu (1826),<br>ceding Assam, Arakan, and Tenasserim,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-539">497</a>; second (1852), Lower Burma<br>(Pegu) annexed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>; third (1885),<br>Upper Burma annexed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-563">521</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Burnes, Sir Alexander, his mission to<br>Kābul (1837), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-542">500</a>; his murder at<br>Kābul (1841), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-542">500</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Bussy, Marquis de, the ablest of Dupleix’s<br>subordinates, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; initiated the policy<br>of subsidiary alliances, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; his defeat<br>of the Marāthās and wise administration of the Northern Circārs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>;<br>taken prisoner at Wandiwāsh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Buxar, decisive battle of (1764), over<br>combined Oudh and Mughal forces by<br>Major Munro, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">C.</h3><p class="index-entry">Cabral, Pedro Alvares, his expedition to<br>India (1500), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>; discovered Brazil,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>; left a factor at Cochin, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Calcutta, founded finally with leave of<br>Aurangzeb (1690) by Job Charnock,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-502">460</a>; capture of (1756) by Sirāj-ud-daula, and the Black Hole, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; recovered by Clive and Watson, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Campbell, Sir Colin. <em>See</em> Clyde, Lord.</p><p class="index-entry">Canning, Lord, Governor-General (1856–<br>62), the last under the Company, the first<br>(and Viceroy) under the Crown, 508–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a>; his equanimity and impartiality<br>during the Mutiny, his ‘clemency’ after<br>it, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-557">515</a>; financial and legal reforms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Carlleyle, Mr. A. C., his studies and observations of prehistoric man, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-132">90</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-133">91</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>; on the enamelled tiles at Agra,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Carlyle, T., his picturesque but inaccurate<br>reference to the Ostend Company, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-507">465</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Carnatic, English and French rivalry and<br>wars in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-513">471</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Carnatic, Nawāb of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-390">348</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cast coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Caste, origin of, in the attitude of Aryan<br>conquerors towards the servile and dark<br>aborigines, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>; the system of<br>three Aryan castes and the Sūdras not<br>marked in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>; in the<br>later Vedas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>; its history in the</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-580"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="580" data-folio="538" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n579_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 538">[p. 538]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Middle Ages, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-348">306</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-349">307</a>; the transition<br>from tribe to caste, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-348">306</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-351">309</a>; protest<br>against, of Buddhism and Jainism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Castro, João de, Portuguese Viceroy<br>(1545-8), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>; relieved Diu and defeated Sultān of Gujarāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>; his fruitless efforts against official peculation<br>and corruption, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>;
