his friendship with<br>Xavier, and encouragement of missionary enterprise, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cavagnari, Sir Louis, murder of, at Kābul,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-560">518</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cave or rock temples, contemporary with<br>the Asoka <em>stūpas</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-203">161</a>; their use or<br>imitation of wooden structures and<br>types, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-204">162</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-205">163</a>; the evolution of the<br><em>chaitya</em>, chapel or shrine, caves, 162–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-206">164</a>; of Jain as well as of Buddhist<br>origin, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-206">164</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Caves, inscriptions on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-89">47</a>; their importance for the history of Western India,<br>in the first and second centuries A. D.,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-89">47</a>; sculptures in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-153">111</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-154">112</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cawnpore, the Mutiny and massacre at,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-554">512</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Ceded and Conquered Provinces,’ 492.</p><p class="index-entry">Celts (arrow-heads, &amp;c.‘), Palaeolithic, of<br>stone, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-133">91</a>; of bone, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-133">91</a>; Neolithic,<br>of stone, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>; copper, ‘bar’ and<br>‘flat,’ found at Gungeria and elsewhere,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-139">97</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-140">98</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Ceylon, Asoka’s mission to, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-326">284</a>; its<br>relations of war and peace with the<br>Pāndyas and Chōlas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-374">332</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-382">340</a>; its coinage imitated from the<br>Chōlas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>; its epigraphy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-104">62</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Chaitya</em> cave-temples, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-204">162</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-206">164</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chalukyas, the, a powerful dynasty of the<br>South, from the fifth century onwards,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-369">327</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-372">330</a>; its extensive conquests south<br>and east under several kings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-369">327</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>;<br>its division into two distinct dynasties,<br>the Western at Bādāmi, the Eastern at<br>Vengi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>; their rare coinage, with<br>boar (<em>varāha</em>) device, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-193">151</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chālukyas, the Eastern or younger branch,<br>of Vengi (615-960), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-372">330</a>; lords<br>of Kalinga and the Eastern Deccan at<br>the end of the eighth century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-372">330</a>;<br>their wars with the Rāshtrakūtas and<br>others, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>; a period (918-73) of<br>anarchy and revolts, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>; the<br>coalition (1070) of the family and kingdom, by marriage or force, with the Chōlas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-377">335</a>; finally conquered (1300)<br>by the Ganpatis of Andhra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-424">382</a>; their<br>coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-193">151</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>; inscriptions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chālukyas, the Western or senior branch,<br>with capital at Bādāmi, later at Kalyān<br>(615-760), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-371">329</a>; their overthrow<br>by the Rāshtrakūtas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-371">329</a>, whom two<br>centuries after (c. 960) their descendant</p><p class="index-entry">Taila II finally overwhelmed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>; the<br>revival of their power, many wars, distinguished kings, and long prosperity<br>for two centuries (960–1160), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-377">335</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-380">338</a>;<br>their subsequent overthrow (1192) by<br>the Yādavas and Hoysalas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-381">339</a>; Chālukyan sculpture and temples, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-165">123</a>;<br>coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>; architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-165">123</a>, 174–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-219">177</a>; in epigraphy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-55">13</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-69">27</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chand Bardāī, the bardic chronicler of<br>Prithwī Rāj, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-346">304</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>; translations of<br>his ballads by Colonel Tod, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chānd Bībī (1596), celebrated queen of<br>Ahmadnagar, repelled Akbar’s attack,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-430">388</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chandels, Rājput clan, of Kālinjar (925–<br>1182), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>; their great temples at<br>Khajrāho, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-166">124</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-221">179</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-222">180</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>; their<br>coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-184">142</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chandernagore, head-quarters of French<br>in Bengal, taken (1757) by Clive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chandragupta (Greek, Sandrokottos,<br>died 297 B.C.), an illegitimate member<br>of the dynasty of Magadha, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>;<br>his alleged meeting in exile with<br>Alexander, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>; headed the native<br>revolt against the Greeks, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>; returned to Magadha and dethroned the<br>Nanda king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>; founded (321) the<br>great Mauryan dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>; induced<br>(312) Seleucus Nicator to relinquish<br>the recovery of the Greek conquests<br>and to concede large tracts, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>; alliance with Seleucus and reception of<br>the embassy of Megasthenes, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>; the<br>great extent of his empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>;<br>the severity of his rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>;<br>a description of his capital at Pātaliputra (now Patna and Bankipore) and<br>palace, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>, of his administration of justice, war, irrigation, and<br>land revenue, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chandragupta I, founder of the Gupta<br>dynasty (A. D. 320), and king of Pātaliputra by his marriage with a Lichchhavi princess, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-332">290</a>; his wide supremacy and dominions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-332">290</a>; established<br>the Gupta era, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-332">290</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chandragupta II, of the Gupta dynasty<br>(A. D. 375–413), annexed Mālwā and<br>Kāthiāwār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-334">292</a>; his poetical epitaph<br>and eulogy on the Iron Pillar at Delhi,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-67">25</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-334">292</a>; his mild, just, and capable<br>administration and capital described by<br>Fa-hien, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-334">292</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-335">293</a>; his encouragement of<br>Buddhism and Sanskrit learning, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-335">293</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Charaka, a great medical authority of<br>the first century A.D., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chariots, in early Indian warfare, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-268">226</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-317">275</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-342">300</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Charnock, Job, chief of the factory<br>at Hooghly, twice (1686-7) repulsed<br>from Calcutta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-502">460</a>; by permission of</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-581"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="581" data-folio="539" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n580_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 539">[p. 539]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Aurangzeb, founded (1690) Calcutta,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-502">460</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chārvāka, the materialistic school of,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-303">261</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chauhāns, a large and powerful Rājput<br>clan round the Sāmbhar Lake, their<br>capital at Ajmer, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>; their short-lived<br>glory, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>; Prithwī Rāj (A.D. 1172–92)<br>their last and greatest king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-357">315</a>; their coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-185">143</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chet Singh, Rājā of Benares, fined by<br>Warren Hastings, rebelled and was<br>defeated, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-526">484</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Child, Sir Josia, predominant in the Court<br>of East India Company, and brother of<br>President of Surat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-501">459</a>; his declaration<br>(1686) of a new and aggressive policy,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-501">459</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-502">460</a>; the feeble action taken in<br>India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-502">460</a>; secured (1693) a new<br>charter by lavish bribery, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-503">461</a>; the<br>parliamentary resolution and inquiry<br>(1694–5), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-503">461</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chiliānwāla, disastrous battle of, in second<br>Sikh War, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chinese, influence on Indian art, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-172">130</a>;<br>translations of Buddhist textbooks, 259–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-302">260</a>; defeat of Kadphises II by (A.D.<br>90), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-331">289</a>; defeat of, by Kanishka,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-331">289</a>; embassy to Harsha, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-343">301</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chinese pilgrims in India. <em>See</em> Fa-hien<br><em>and</em> Hiuen-tsiang.</p><p class="index-entry">Chingīz Khān (1162–1227), the indirect<br>pressure on India of his conquests, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-400">358</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chīnī-kā-Rauza, a poet, tomb of, at Agra,<br>encaustic tiles on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-171">129</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chinsura, head-quarters of Dutch in<br>Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; successfully attacked by<br>Clive (1759), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-494">452</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chitor, two towers of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-165">123</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-166">124</a>; Sesodia<br>(Gahlot) Rājputs of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>; the fame of its<br>sieges, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-360">318</a>; attacked by Ibrāhīm Lodī,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-409">367</a>; by Akbar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>; the poem based on<br>its capture (1303) by Alā-ud-dīn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-473">431</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chitrāl, rising at, and siege (1895), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-567">525</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chōlas, the, with capital at Kānchī, their<br>defeat (c. 930) of the Pāndyas and<br>Singhalese, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-374">332</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>; their king,<br>Rājārājā, took Kalinga and conquered<br>(1002) Ceylon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>; repulsed from<br>Western Chālukya country, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-378">336</a>;<br>conquered the Vengi country and restored the dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>; their final<br>coalition by peace or war with the<br>Eastern Chālukyas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>; the joint<br>dynasty (1070) paramount throughout<br>the South to the end of the twelfth<br>century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-377">335</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-381">339</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-382">340</a>; finally<br>merged in Vijayanagar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-386">344</a>; their<br>coinage with the Pāndyan fish and<br>Chālukyan boar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>; the ‘standing<br>king’ device on the coins of Rājārājā,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>; inscriptions of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-54">12</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Christianity, its possible influence on the newer Buddhism and its art, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-155">113</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-156">114</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-157">115</a>; on Hinduism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-456">414</a>; the large<br>obligations to, of Kabīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-458">416</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-459">417</a>, of<br>Tulsī Dās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-460">418</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Chronology: the difficulties of early, due<br>to the innate inexactness of early narrators, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-45">3</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-47">5</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-249">207</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-313">271</a>; their neglect<br>of inscriptions, &amp;c., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a> <em>et passim</em>; the absence of dates in early documents, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-58">16</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>; the habit of regarding contemporary dynasties as successive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-51">9</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-57">15</a>; the value of inscriptions in their<br>correction and determination, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>;<br>the establishment of synchronisms between events in India and in countries<br>of known history, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-313">271</a>; the complication of dating by different eras, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>;<br>modern knowledge of their initial points<br>and ready methods of computation of<br>dates, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-107">65</a>; the chronology of the<br>Gandhāra school and Ajantā paintings,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-157">115</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-160">118</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-162">120</a>. <em>See also</em> Dates.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Chronology of India</em>, by Miss Duff, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-108">66</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Cinder-mounds,’ of Southern India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>;<br>doubtful whether remains of recent<br>sacrifices of women, or neolithic of<br>animals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Civil Service, purification and reorganization of by Clive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>, Warren Hastings,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-523">481</a>; thrown open to competition (1853),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-556">514</a>; the Provincial Service (1887), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-564">522</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Clay, variously inscribed tablets of,<br>usually votive and Buddhist, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-76">34</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-79">37</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-80">38</a>;<br>clay seals from Sunet, as votive offerings<br>(not Buddhist), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-80">38</a>; clay seals of<br>records, &amp;c., large recent discoveries of,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-80">38</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-81">39</a>; seal of and inscription on terra-cotta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-81">39</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-82">40</a>; inscribed bricks and<br>tablets, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-82">40</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Clive, Robert, 1st Lord (1725–74), the<br>military founder of the Indian Empire,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-523">481</a>; taken prisoner at Madras (1746)<br>and escaped to Fort St. David, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-513">471</a>;<br>at the siege of Pondicherry (1748) under<br>Stringer Lawrence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; took (1751)<br>and defended Arcot, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; invalided<br>(1753–5) to England, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; the Black<br>Hole (1756) of Calcutta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; recapture (1757) of Calcutta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>; battle of<br>Plassey and its results (1757), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-519">477</a>;<br>Clive’s <em>jāgīr</em> (1759) or military fief,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-519">477</a>; Governor of Bengal (1758–60),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>; dispersed the forces of Shāh Alam,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>; sent Colonel Forde (1759) to the<br>Northern Circārs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>, to co-operate<br>with Coote against the French, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>;<br>defeated the Dutch by land and sea,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>; in England (1760–5), mismanagement during his absence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>;<br>his second Governorship, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; his<br>attempt to purify and reorganize the<br>Company’s civil and military service,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; the civil resistance and military <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-582" data-page="582" data-folio="540" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n581_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 540">[p. 540]</button> mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; his partition (1765) of<br>the Gangetic valley, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; Clive’s dual<br>system of the Nizāmat or criminal<br>jurisdiction of Bengal vested in the<br>Nawāb, the Dīwānī (revenue and civil)<br>in the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>, its subsequent failure<br>and repeal by Hastings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-523">481</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-525">483</a>; the<br>parliamentary inquiry (1773) into Indian<br>affairs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>; the vote of censure and of<br>recognition of his merits, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-522">480</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Clyde, Lord, relief of Lucknow, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-555">513</a>;<br>campaign in Oudh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-555">513</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Coen, great Dutch Governor-General,<br>founded the city of Batavia, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-494">452</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Coinage of the East India Company and<br>other European powers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-190">148</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>. <em>See</em><br><em>also</em> under Numismatics, and several<br>Sovereigns and Dynasties.</p><p class="index-entry">Coins, ancient, their use a necessity of<br>foreign commerce, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-177">135</a>; a product of<br>private enterprise, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-177">135</a>; punched, cast,<br>died, and double died, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-181">139</a>;<br>the characteristic Indian square form,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-187">145</a>; their early materials, copper,<br>silver, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-180">138</a>; lead coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>; the<br>first gold coins of the Kushān dynasty, and attachment of their leaves, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>;<br>their seals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-75">33</a>; generally found in<br>private hands, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-69">27</a>; often found, like<br>other portable records and articles, in<br>localities distant from their place of<br>origin or reference, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-69">27</a>, how therein<br>they differ from stone inscriptions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-69">27</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-70">28</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-77">35</a>; often called ‘grants’ by epigraphists and distinguished (wrongly)<br>from ‘inscriptions’ on stones, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-70">28</a>; the<br>native terms by which they call themselves, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-70">28</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>; royal autographs, signmanuals, or images in place of or addition to authenticating seals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-75">33</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Copper Age, the, intervening in India<br>between Stone and Iron Ages, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-139">97</a>;<br>copper, a common metal of the country,<br>and in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-140">98</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>; the great<br>and important find (1870) of copper<br>instruments and celts at Gungeria and<br>elsewhere, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-139">97</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-140">98</a>; their resemblance<br>to Irish and other specimens, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-139">97</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-140">98</a>; the<br>occurrence with them of plates of silver,<br>not a common metal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-139">97</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-140">98</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Corinthian capitals, of Gandhāra sculptures, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-155">113</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-208">166</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">138; the octagonal form in Assam, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>; their weight based in North India on the weight of the <em>ratī</em> seed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>; in the South on the weight of the <em>kalanju</em> seed or ‘Molucca bean,’ 151; the reasons of their scarcity in the South, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-192">150</a>; the change to, from barter, through jewellery, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-268">226</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Colbert, founded Compagnie des Indes (1674), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Colophons, and introductions to literary works, the value of the dates and items contained in them, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>; instances of their inaccuracy and incompleteness, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Colossal Jain statues, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-164">122</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Commerce and Industry, Department of, instituted (1904), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Compagnie des Indes (Colbert’s), founded (1674) Pondicherry, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>; finally absorbed by Law’s Company of the West, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-506">464</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Compagnie d’Orient (Richelieu’s), its failure to colonize (1642) Madagascar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Coorg, annexed (1834) by unanimous wish of its people, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-540">498</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-541">499</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Coote, Sir Eyre, defeated Lally at Wandi-wāsh (1760), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-495">453</a>; took Pondicherry and Gingee (1761), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>; the final blows to French power in India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-516">474</a>; his last victories (1781) at Porto Novo and Sholinghar in the war with Haidar Alī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Copper, inscriptions on sheets of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-346">304</a>; some commemorative and dedicatory, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>, most donative charters, grants, or title-deeds, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-69">27</a>; their size, the number</p><p class="index-entry">Cornwallis, Lord, twice Governor-General (1786–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-135">93</a>, 1805), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-530">488</a>, established the Appellate Court of Criminal Judicature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>; separated the functions of District Collector and Judge, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>; his and Sir John Shore’s Permanent Settlement of Bengal (1793), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>; the previous methods of settling and collecting revenue, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>; the third Mysore War (1790–3) conducted by the Governor-General and defeat of Tipū, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>; his second short tenure of office, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cosmogony, of the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-260">218</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-261">219</a>; of the Purāṇas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-278">236</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Courten, Sir William, his Association, founded (1635) by a grant from Charles I, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-500">458</a>; its mutually disastrous rivalry with the Old Company, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-500">458</a>; their union (1657) under a new charter, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-500">458</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cow, the, its early sanctity, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-258">216</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-259">217</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>; a standard of value in barter, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-268">226</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cowell (and Thomas), translation of the <em>Harshacharita</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>; on St. Augustine and <em>bhakti</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-456">414</a>; calls Makunda Rām the Indian Crabbe, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-468">426</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cremation, practice of, probably subsequent to that of burial, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>; of Iron (not of Neolithic) Age, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>; coexistence of both forms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>; became the rule in Vedic times, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-260">218</a>; its ritual and <em>satī</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-260">218</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Criminal procedure, Lord Ripon’s amendment of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-561">519</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-562">520</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cromwell, Oliver, wrested (1654) from the Portuguese the right of England to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-583" data-page="583" data-folio="541" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n582_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 541">[p. 541]</button> trade in the East, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-498">456</a>; exacted (1524)<br>indemnity from the Dutch for the massacre of Amboyna, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-499">457</a>; saved (1657)<br>the Company from ruin by a new<br>charter, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-500">458</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Crystal, only one record inscribed on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-78">36</a>;<br>crystal phials and caskets, associated<br>with or containing records, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-78">36</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-79">37</a>;<br>description of crystal bowl at Piprahwa,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-175">133</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cunha, Nuno da, Portuguese Viceroy<br>(1529–38), first opened up trade with<br>Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; the settlement at Hooghly,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; his further designs thwarted by<br>the siege of Diu by the Turkish and<br>Egyptian fleet, and the Sultān of<br>Gujarāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; his death in a Portuguese prison before their defeat by sea<br>and land, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Cup- and ring-marks, prehistoric objects,<br>probably neolithic, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Curzon of Kedleston, Lord, Viceroy<br>(1898–1905), the North-West frontier<br>policy, a compromise between the Lawrence and Forward schools, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-568">526</a>; the<br>formation (1901) of a new North-West<br>Frontier Province, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-569">527</a>; the Tibet mission (1904) to Lhasa, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-569">527</a>; plague and<br>famine, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-569">527</a>; improvement in finances,<br>and creation (1900) of gold reserve<br>fund, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>; remissions and reductions<br>of land and salt tax, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>; the Punjab<br>Land Alienation Act, Agricultural<br>banks (1904), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>; reorganization of<br>Universities, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>; the check to the<br>‘perfect and pernicious’ system of reports in the Indian Civil Service, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>;<br>army and police reforms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-570">528</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>;<br>the co-operation of the Feudatory<br>States in administration eulogized and<br>stimulated, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>; Coronation <em>darbār</em><br>(1903), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>; the partition (1905) of<br>Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>; the grounds of his resignation (1905) during his second term<br>of office, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-572">530</a>; his encouragement of archaeology, 101 and <em>n.</em></p><p class="index-entry">Customs: inland lines, abolished by Lord<br>Mayo, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a>; many import duties abolished by Lord Ripon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-562">520</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-563">521</a>.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">D.</h3><p class="index-entry">Dādū, a cotton-cleaner of Ahmadābād,<br>seventeenth-century follower of Kabīr,<br>and founder of a sect, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-459">417</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Dalhousie, Lord, Governor-General<br>(1848–56), the greatest of Indian proconsuls since Warren Hastings and<br>Wellesley, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>; completed the fabric<br>of British rule in India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-550">508</a>; his administrative reforms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>; inauguration<br>of the Indian railway system and of the<br>Public Works department, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>; second<br>Sikh War (1848–9) and annexation and pacification of the Punjab, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>;<br>second Burmese War (1852) and annexation of Lower Burma, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>; his<br>policy towards the Native States, of<br>legitimate annexation for their own<br>good, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-548">506</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-550">508</a>; his doctrine of ‘lapse,’<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-548">506</a>; ‘lapsed’ States, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-548">506</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-549">507</a>; Oudh<br>annexed for misgovernment, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-549">507</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-550">508</a>;<br>his policy one of the causes of the<br>Mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-551">509</a>, and renounced in the<br>Queen’s Proclamation of 1858, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-557">515</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Dāmodara-Miśra, a dramatist of the<br>eleventh century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-291">249</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Danes, the, their Companies and settlements, at Tranquebar (1620), at Serampore (1676), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-506">464</a>; the Danish Company of 1728, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-508">466</a>; all purchased (1845)<br>by British Government, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-506">464</a>; Indo-Danish coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Darius, conquest of North-Western India<br>by, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-314">272</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-315">273</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Dāsas or Dasyus, ‘fiends,’ name given in<br>the Rigveda to the aborigines by the<br>Aryas and ‘kinsmen,’ also Kṛishṇas<br>‘black-skins,’ 221.</p><p class="index-entry">Dates: the absence or inaccuracy and inexactness of, in the Vaṁśāvalis, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>;<br>in the Purāṇas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-57">15</a>; in the <em>Rājataraṁgiṇī</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-58">16</a>; in quasi-historical literature,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>; their presence and value in<br>introductions and colophons, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>;<br>their first regular appearance in inscriptions of the first century <span class="sc">B. C.</span>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>; the<br>different eras, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-107">65</a>;
