their initial<br>points now well-known, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>; the computation of Hindu and Muhammadan dates<br>by keys and tables, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-106">64</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-107">65</a>; their absence from South Indian coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-192">150</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Daughters, undesired in the Vedas, as<br>now, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-267">225</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Dāyabhāga</em>, a famous treatise on the law<br>of inheritance, translated by Colebrooke, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-304">262</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Deccan, the, the home of philosophy<br>and source of Rājput civilization, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-358">316</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-363">321</a>. <em>See also under</em> Southern India.</p><p class="index-entry">Delhi, Prithwī Rāj its last (1192) Hindu<br>king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; thirty-four Muhammadan kings of (1206–1526), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-399">357</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-410">368</a>;<br>their five houses, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-399">357</a>; successions generally contested, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-399">357</a>; tables of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-410">368</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-411">369</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Delhi city, sack of and massacre by<br>Taimūr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-408">366</a>, and by Nādir Shāh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-451">409</a>;<br>taken by Bābar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>, by Afghāns (1757),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-452">410</a>; occupied by<br>Lord Lake, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>; siege and storm of,<br>during the Mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-555">513</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Demetrius, Graeco-Bactrian king (c. <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a><br><span class="sc">B. its<br>brick Hindu architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-230">188</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-231">189</a>; the rise of the cults of<br>Vishnu and Siva, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>; the final fall of Buddhism,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Historical romances in prose and verse,<br>in Sanskrit, Tamil, and Pāli, instances<br>of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-60">18</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-283">241</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-310">268</a>; sources and instances of error in<br>his statement of distances, &amp;c., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-121">79</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-123">81</a>;<br>his pilgrimage in India to all the holy<br>places of Buddhism, and collection of<br>manuscripts and images, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-338">296</a>; his visit<br>(643) by command to the court of<br>Harsha, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-72">30</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-338">296</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>; his presence at<br>the Council of Kanauj, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>, at the<br>‘salvation’ assembly at Prayāg, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>;<br>conspiracy of Brāhmans against him<br>and his royal patron, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>; his pleasing<br>picture of the political, social, and<br>religious state of India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-341">299</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-342">300</a>; his<br>(and Fa-hien’s) extraordinary statement<br>of the absence of a gold and silver<br>coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-342">300</a>; his return home and<br>translation of the scriptures, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-339">297</a>; in<br>the South, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-372">330</a>; a Tamil chronicle (of last century) of Mysore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/01-frontmatter/#page-6">6</a>; fanciful, uncritical, inexact, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/01-frontmatter/#page-6">6</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kṛishṇa, the cult and lyric literature of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-463">421</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-467">425</a>; a deity in the Mahābhārata, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-463">421</a>; founded as a popular religion and literature by Vallabhāchārya in the sixteenth century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-463">421</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-464">422</a>; his successors in various dialects and in Dravidian, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-464">422</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-467">425</a>; compared with the cult of Rāma, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-464">422</a>; its symbolical sexualism often misunderstood and degraded, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-464">422</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kṛishṇa, ‘black-skins,’ name given by the Aryans to the aborigines, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kṛishṇa Miśra (A.D. 1100), the author of a powerful allegorical play, ‘The Rise of the Moon of Knowledge,’ 249, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-292">250</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kṛishṇadēva, the greatest of the Vijayanagar Rāyas (1509–30), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-388">346</a>; his successes against the Muhammadans, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-388">346</a>; his patronage of Telugu literature and learning, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-479">437</a>; himself an author, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-479">437</a>; his friendship towards the Portuguese, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-388">346</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kshattriyas, the warrior caste, in Rigvedic period not yet hereditary or a caste, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>, its gradual specialization, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>; recognized in the Yajurveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>; the popular legend of their extinction by the Brāhmans, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-350">308</a>; still kings down to the seventh century, their place since taken by Rājputs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-350">308</a>; now as a caste conspicuous by its absence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-365">323</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kshemīśvara, dramatic poet of the tenth century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-291">249</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kumāragupta I, Mahendra (A.D. 413–455), defeated by the White Huns, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Kurral</em>, the great Tamiḷ poem of Tiruvaḷḷuvar, a Pariah, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kurukshetra, the territory of the Kurus, the holy land of the Yajurveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-269">227</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Kushān (Indo-Scythian) dynasty and power, the union (A.D. 45) of the Yueh-chi horde under, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>; href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-76">34</a>, on other substances than metals,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-76">34</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-91">49</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mathematics, the great achievements of<br>the Indians in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a> ; the inventors of<br>our figures and decimal system, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a> ;<br>their progress in arithmetic, algebra,<br>geometry, and astronomy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a> ; their<br>works composed in verse, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mathurā (Muttra), sculpture of the<br>Kushān period excavated at, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-152">110</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-157">115</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mauryan dynasty, the, founded (321-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-226">184</a><br>B. C.) by Chandragupta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-322">280</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-328">286</a> ; its<br>intimate relations of diplomacy, commerce, and art with the West, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-148">106</a> ;<br>the Mauryan period of Indian art, 104-<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-154">112</a> ; has no known coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-180">138</a> ;<br>architecture of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-209">167</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-210">168</a>. <em>See also under</em><br>Asoka, Bimbisāra, Chandragupta.</p><p class="index-entry">Mausoleums and tombs, architecturally<br>notable : (Pathān) of Altamsh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a> ; of Tughlak I, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a> ; of Sher Shāh<br>Sūri at Sasarām, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a> ; of Hoshang<br>Shāh at Māndu, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-228">186</a> ; of the Bahmani<br>kings near Gulbarga, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-236">194</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-237">195</a> ; of<br>Mubārak Saiyid and others in Gujarāt,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-238">196</a> ; Gol Gumbaz or great tomb at<br>Bijāpur of Muhammad Ādil Shāh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a> ;<br>the Tāj Mahal at Agra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-169">127</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a> ;<br>of Akbar at Sikandra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-169">127</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a> ; of<br>Jahāngīr near Lahore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a> ; of<br>Humāyūn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a> ; of I‘timād-ud-daula at<br>Agra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a> ; of Aurangzeb’s favourite<br>wife, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a> ; of the poet Chīnī-kā-Rauza<br>near Agra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-171">129</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Māyā</em>, or ‘ cosmic illusion,’ producing the<br>multiplicity of phenomena, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-296">254</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mayo, Lord, Viceroy (1869-72) ; the<br>Ambālā <em>darbār</em> (1869), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a> ; visit (1869-70) of the Duke of Edinburgh,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a> ; his system of Provincial Finance<br>and of local self-government, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a> ;<br>reform of salt duties, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a> ; extension<br>of public works, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a> ; assassination by<br>a convict in the Andamans, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-559">517</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Medicine, literature of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a> ; Charaka of<br>the first century and the great surgeon<br>Suśruta of the second, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a> ; its translation into Arabic in the eighth century,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a> ; rhinoplasty, or the art of forming<br>artificial noses, its valuable contribution<br>to surgery, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-308">266</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Megalithic tombs, of the Iron Age, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Megasthenes, envoy of Seleucus to the<br>court of Chandragupta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a> ; his careful and precise report on the country<br>and court of his mission, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-249">207</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Meghadūta</em>, or ‘ Cloud Messenger,’ a lyric<br>poem of Kālidāsa, admired by Goethe,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-284">242</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Menander, Graeco-Bactrian king, ruler of<br>Kābul, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a> ; his invasion (153 B. C.)<br>of India as far as Oudh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a> ; his defeat<br>by the Sunga king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Metcalfe, Sir Charles (Lord), Governor-General (1835-6), his mission to Ranjīt<br>Singh (1809), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a> ; gave liberty<br>to the press, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-541">499</a> ; one of Wellesley’s<br>three ‘ politicals,’ 493.</p><p class="index-entry">Metres and accents, Vedic, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-252">210</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-253">211</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Michelborne, Sir Edward, his licence<br>(1604) to trade in the East from<br>James I, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-497">455</a> ; his piratical and injurious proceedings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-497">455</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Midnall, John (1599-1606), London<br>merchant, his overland journey and<br>stay of seven years in the East, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-496">454</a> ;<br>his visit to and favours from Akbar,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-496">454</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mihirakula, White Hun chief, defeated<br>by the Gupta king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a> ; his cruel<br>tyranny in Kashmīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a> ; in records<br>misdated by 1100 years, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-58">16</a> ; his defeat<br>recorded in epigraphy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-92">50</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mīmāṃsā, the, a practical (orthodox)<br>system in contrast with the speculative<br>Vedānta, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-297">255</a> ; its textbooks and commentators, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-297">255</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Minarets (<em>minārs</em>), their first use for the<br><em>mu‘azzin</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-226">184</a> ; the great <em>minār</em> and<br>others at Gaur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-233">191</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-235">193</a> ; the<br>Kutb Minār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-168">126</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Miniature painting, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-173">131</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Minto, Lord, Governor-General (1807-<br>13), carried on the imposed policy of<br>non-intervention, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a> ; the embassies of the ‘ political ’ Metcalfe, Elphinstone, and Malcolm to the Punjab,<br>Afghānistān, and Persia, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Minto, Lord, succeeded (1905) Lord<br>Curzon as Viceroy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-572">530</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-598"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="598" data-folio="556" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n597_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 556">[p. 556]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Mīrā Bāī, princess of Mewār and popular<br>(Krishna) poetess, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-466">424</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Miriam’s House,’ at Fatehpur Sīkri, the<br>so-called ‘Annunciation’ at, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-172">130</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mīr Jafar, conspirator against his master,<br>Sirāj-ud-daulā, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>; placed on his<br>throne by Clive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>; dethroned and<br>restored, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mīr Kāsim, son-in-law of Mīr Jafar,<br>made (1761) Nawāb in his place, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>;<br>his grants of money and territory, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>;<br>his intrigues and rising (1763) against<br>the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>; the massacre of<br>sepoys and of English at Patna, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>;<br>defeated by Major Adams and fled to<br>Oudh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>; the combined forces of<br>Oudh and the Mughal, Shāh Alam,<br>defeated (1764) by Major Munro at<br>Baxār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Missionaries, of Buddhism, sent out by<br>Asoka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-326">284</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Mitāksharā</em>, a famous commentary on<br>the code of Yājñavalkya, partly translated by Colebrooke, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-304">262</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mongols, invasions by, of 1223, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-400">358</a>; in<br>Multān and Sind, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-402">360</a>; defeated by<br>Balban’s son, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-403">361</a>; advanced to Delhi<br>and were defeated and finally expelled<br>from India and Multān and Sind by<br>Alā-ud-dīn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-404">362</a>; bought off by Muhammad Tughlak, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-406">364</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Monson, Colonel, his disastrous retreat<br>(1804) before Holkar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mosaic or inlay, its use in Muhammadan<br>and Mughal architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-168">126</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mosques, early, converted or modified<br>Hindu or Jain temples, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>; the introduction of the arch and development of<br>a new style, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>; the phases of<br>elaboration, simplicity, and of a return<br>to ornamentation between the thirteenth<br>and fifteenth centuries, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-226">184</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mosques, architecturally notable (Pathān),<br>at Ajmer, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>; the mosque of Kutb<br>Minār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-168">126</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>; of Kalān at<br>Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>; (Sharkī) the Jāmi, Atala,<br>and Lāl Darwāza, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-226">184</a>; (Mālwā) the<br>great Jāmi Masjid and others at Māndu, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-227">185</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-228">186</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-229">187</a>; the Adīna Masjid,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-231">189</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>, the Eklākhi mosque or tomb,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>, and others at Pandua, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>; the<br>Kadam Rasūl Masjid, Tāntipāra, and<br>Lattan, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-233">191</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-234">192</a>; the Khwāja-kī and<br>the Sonā at Gaur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-234">192</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-235">193</a>; the great<br>mosque at Gulbarga, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-235">193</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-236">194</a>; at<br>Bīdar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-236">194</a>; at Bījāpur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>; Sher<br>Shāh’s near Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>; Akbar’s great<br>mosque at Fatehpur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-169">127</a>; Jahāngīr’s<br>at Lahore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-170">128</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>; Shāhjahān’s Jāmi<br>Masjid at Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>; Motī Masjid at<br>Agra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Moulds or matrices of seals, rock-cut, of<br>steatite, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-90">48</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-91">49</a>; matrix of early seventh century found at Rōhtāsgaṛh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-91">49</a>; probably only used for very soft materials,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-91">49</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mother-of-pearl inlay on marble, at<br>Ahmadābād, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-168">126</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mountains mentioned in the Rigveda,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-262">220</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Mricchakaṭikā</em>, a serio-comic play, attributed to king Śūdraka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-289">247</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Mudrā-rākshasa</em>, a Sanskrit play (A. D.<br>800) of political intrigue, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-291">249</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mughal empire, the (1526–1803), 393–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>; two hundred years of strong<br>government and increasing order, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-435">393</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>; its success due to the moral and<br>physical capacity and long reigns of its<br>first rulers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-435">393</a>; the concentration of<br>power in their own hands, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-435">393</a>; the<br>continuity of their policy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-435">393</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a>;<br>its decay due to weak rulers, an unwieldy empire and consequent delegation of powers, and the revival of Hindu<br>power, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a>; disputed successions<br>not a factor, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-435">393</a>; its fall before the<br>British power after the battle (1764) of<br>Buxar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>; its patronage of art,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-168">126</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-174">132</a>; its coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-188">146</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-190">148</a>; architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-243">201</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Mughal Saracenic architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-243">201</a>;<br>its unity and historical completeness,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>; its earlier examples neglected and<br>ill-treated under British rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>;<br>its vigorous development under Akbar,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>; after Akbar, outgrew Hindu influence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-241">199</a>; its magnificence and refinement under Shāh Jahān, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>; its<br>natural decline under Aurangzeb, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Muhammad Ghorī (of Ghor) (1186–<br>1206), the lieutenant and finally successor of his brother, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; established by thirty years’ war an enduring<br>Muhammadan kingdom from Peshāwar<br>to the Bay of Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; his<br>earlier (1175–86) campaigns and conquests in Multān, Sind, and Lahore,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>; his later (1191–1206), wider, and<br>more rapid conquests, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>; Prithwī<br>Rāj, last independent Hindu king of<br>Delhi, first victorious, finally defeated,<br>slain, and his territory annexed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; capital removed (1193) to Delhi,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; general overthrow of Rājput clans<br>and kingdoms by Muhammad and his<br>lieutenant Qutb-ud-dīn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>; murdered (1206) by Muhammadan heretics,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>; the practical independence after<br>his death of his satraps, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>; his<br>Indian coinage of billon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-186">144</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Muhammad Shāh, ‘Ādil, Sūr emperor of<br>Delhi (1554–6), murdered and succeeded his nephew, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-438">396</a>; illiterate and<br>incompetent, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>; his reign a struggle<br>for the throne between relatives, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-438">396</a>; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-599" data-page="599" data-folio="557" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n598_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 557">[p. 557]</button> his Hindu general and minister, Hīmū,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-438">396</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-439">397</a>.<br>Muhammad Shāh, Mughal emperor<br>(1719-48), set up by the Saiyids, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-449">407</a>;<br>their unpopularity and overthrow, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-449">407</a>;<br>the rise of independent states, and the<br>encroachments of the Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-449">407</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-450">408</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-451">409</a>; saved her <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-602" data-page="602" data-folio="560" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n601_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 560">[p. 560]</button> husband from his chief general, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-442">400</a>;<br>her name with his on his coins, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-189">147</a>.<br>Nūrmahal, the sculptured front of the<br><em>sarai at</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-174">132</a>.<br>Nyāya, an orthodox system of philosophy<br>or formal logic, including the syllogism,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-297">255</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>; still the basis of philosophical studies in India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>.</p><h4 class="sec-h3">O.</h4><p class="index-entry">Occupations and amusements in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-268">226</a>.<br>Ochterlony, General, his campaigns in<br>Nepāl (1814-5), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-536">494</a>.<br>Official records, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-54">12</a>; the necessity of<br>their existence and preservation in<br>Hindu administration, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>; of copies of<br>orders, treaties, alliances, local reports,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-54">12</a>; of tribute and revenue, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-54">12</a>;<br>probably in form of day-books, like the<br>Diaries of the Pēshwās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-54">12</a>.<br>Omichand, Clive's fictitious treaty with,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>.<br>Orissa, <em>vaṁśāvalis</em> of, going back from<br>A.D. 1871 to 3102 B.C., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-52">10</a>, utterly fanciful and misleading, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>; their purpose to magnify the temple of Jagannātha at Purī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>; instances of their<br>gross errors, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>; its real history begins<br>A.D. 78, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>; caves in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-206">164</a>; old temples<br>in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-166">124</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-222">180</a>.<br>Orissa Province, acquired and reduced<br>(1575-92) by Akbar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-440">398</a>, ceded (1802)<br>by the Bhonsla, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>; the famine of<br>(1866), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a>.<br>Oriyā literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-474">432</a>.<br>Ostend Company, the, incorporated<br>(1722) by Charles VI, the Holy<br>Roman emperor, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-506">464</a>; its settlements<br>in Madras and Bengal between English<br>and Dutch settlements, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-507">465</a>; its objects<br>not only commercial but also part of<br>a world-policy of German maritime<br>power, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-507">465</a>; the opposition of the<br>maritime powers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-507">465</a>; sacrificed (1727)<br>to the Pragmatic Sanction, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-507">465</a>; its<br>settlements destroyed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-508">466</a>; the failure<br>of subsequent (1728-84) attempts to<br>revive it or its aims by appeals to other<br>powers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-508">466</a>; the causes of German<br>failure, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-510">468</a>.<br>Oudh, Rājputs of, mostly Aryanized Sūdras, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-350">308</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-355">313</a>; conquered (1194) by Muhammad Ghorī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; under the Mughals<br>(1527), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>; its practical independence<br>(1721) under its Nawāb Wazīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-449">407</a>; reduced to submission (1764) by the battle<br>of Buxar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>; sale by Hastings (1773)<br>of Allahābād and Korā to the Nawāb,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-525">483</a>; the Nawāb with British help defeats<br>and expels the Rohillas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-525">483</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-526">484</a>; the<br>grounds of Hastings's action, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-525">483</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-526">484</a>;<br>the fine imposed by Hastings on the</p><p class="index-entry">Begam for aiding rebellion, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-526">484</a>; cession of the Doāb to Wellesley, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-531">489</a>;<br>annexed (1856) for misgovernment by<br>Lord Dalhousie, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-549">507</a>.</p><h4 class="sec-h3">P.</h4><p class="index-entry"><em>Padumāwati</em>, the, an allegorico-historical<br>poem by Malik Muhammad (1540),<br>written in Eastern Hindī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-472">430</a>; based<br>on the siege and capture (1303) of Chitor<br>by Alā-ud-dīn, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-473">431</a>.<br>Pagodas, coins of Southern India, 150-<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-195">153</a>.<br>Paintings, of the caves of Ajantā, the only<br>ancient Indian, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-159">117</a>; their technique,<br>age, subjects, and merits considered,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-159">117</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-161">119</a>; their origin (probably Persian) and affinities discussed, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-161">119</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-163">121</a>;<br>paintings of the early Mughal period,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-171">129</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-172">130</a>; Akbar's patronage and failure<br>to found a national school, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-172">130</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-173">131</a>.<br>Paithan plates, the, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-75">33</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-76">34</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-102">60</a>.<br>Pāl (Buddhist) dynasty (A.D. 900-1000)<br>of Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-358">316</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>.<br>Palaces, halls, gateways, &amp;c., architecturally notable, the Jahāz Mahal and<br>Hindola palaces, and many others at<br>Māndu, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-228">186</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-229">187</a>; the Alai Darwāza<br>(gateway), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>; the Dākhil or Salāmī<br>gateway at Gaur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>; Kotwālī Darwāza (gateway) at Gaur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-234">192</a>; at<br>Bīdar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-236">194</a>; at Bījāpur, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>; the <em>sarai</em><br>at Nūrmahal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-174">132</a>; palaces of Shāhjahān at Agra and Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>.<br>Palaeography, its inquiry brought up to<br>date in Bühler's <em>Indische Palaeographie</em><br>(1896), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-115">73</a>, now needing revision and<br>addition, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-115">73</a>; the methods of further<br>progress, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-115">73</a>, and its needs, especially<br>of facsimiles of the inscriptions, and<br>coins in their varieties, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-115">73</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-116">74</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-117">75</a>.<br>Palaeolithic Age, the, and its rare remains, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>; rude stone implements<br>of a race of which there are no skulls<br>or skeletons, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>; contemporary with<br>extinct animals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-133">91</a>; without tombs or<br>pottery, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>; the great gulf of time<br>between it and the Neolithic Age,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-132">90</a>; no evidence of continuity between palaeolithic men and the subsequent population, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-132">90</a>.<br>Pāli, the sacred language of southern<br>Buddhism, its relation to Prākrit and<br>Sanskrit, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-250">208</a>; its sacred canon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>.<br><em>See also under</em> Buddhism.<br>Pallāvaram, prehistoric tombs at, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>.<br>Pallavas, the, a powerful and for some<br>time paramount nation of the South<br>(from c. A.D. 400 to 977), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-367">325</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a><br><em>passim</em>; their final downfall, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>; their<br>capital, Kāñchī, twice taken and once<br>spared by the Western Chālukyas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-371">329</a>; in epigraphy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-603"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="603" data-folio="561" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n602_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 561">[p. 561]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Pañchālas, with the Kurus, the leading<br>peoples of the Brāhmaṇa period, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-264">222</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-265">223</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-277">235</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Pañchatantra</em>, a collection of fables about<br>animals, written in Kashmīr by a<br>Vishnuite Brāhman in the second century B.C., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-292">250</a>; its healthy humour and<br>satire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-293">251</a>; a history of its text and<br>translations and wide influence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-292">250</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-293">251</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pandua, the capital (1345) after Gaur of<br>the Muhammadan kingdom of Bengal,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-231">189</a>; its architectural remains, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-231">189</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-232">190</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pāṇḍyas, the, of Madura, their attack<br>(c. 850) on Ceylon and sack of its<br>capital, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>; the tables turned (c. 870),<br>Madura taken and plundered, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>; later<br>alliance between Madura and Ceylon<br>against the Chōlas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-373">331</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-374">332</a>; the Chōlas claim to have taken Madura and<br>invaded Ceylon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-374">332</a>; their rule at<br>Madura till the end of the twelfth century, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-382">340</a>; overthrown (1310) by Malik<br>Kāfūr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-385">343</a>; Madura a province of the<br>Vijayanagar empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-386">344</a>; their coinage and fish emblem, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-192">150</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>;
