in epigraphy, 12 (<em>n.</em>).</p><p class="index-entry">Pāṇini (c. 300 B.C.), his grammar, the<br>shortest and fullest in the world, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-275">233</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-305">263</a>; its commentaries and adaptations,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-305">263</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pānīpat, decisive battles at: defeat (1526)<br>of Ibrāhīm Lodī, by Bābar, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-436">394</a>; of<br>Afghāns (1556) by Akbar's general,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-439">397</a>; of imperial troops (1739) by<br>Nādir Shāh, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-450">408</a>; of Marāthās (1761)<br>by the Afghāns and Muhammadans of<br>the North, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Panjābī, its wealth of ballad-poetry, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-472">430</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pariahs, represent the old Dravidian stock,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-365">323</a>; the Tamil Pariah poet and poetess,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Parthian domination on north-west frontier of India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-331">289</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pātaliputra, capital of the Mauryan<br>dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-146">104</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-323">281</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-324">282</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-334">292</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pathān or Turkī dynasties of Delhi,<br>Muhammadan dynasties before the<br>Mughals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-223">181</a>; their characteristic<br>architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-224">182</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Patna massacre (1763), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Paṭṭāvalis</em>, or successions of Jain pontiffs,<br>going back to 527 B.C., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>; probably<br>compiled in ninth century A.D., <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-46">4</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>;<br>based on more ancient and correct<br>lists, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pedigrees and successions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>; their<br>preservation, a national characteristic,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>; their abundance in legal transactions<br>of to-day, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>; of administrative families,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>; of ordinary cultivators, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>; of religious colleges, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>; of kings and royal families, of great antiquity, inexact, and to be used cautiously, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-50">8</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-53">11</a>;<br>the frequent insertion of pedigrees and<br>genealogical details in royal and other<br>grants, religious and secular, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-103">61</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-104">62</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Periplus, the, of the Erythraean Sea, its<br>geography, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-118">76</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-119">77</a>; the result of personal experience of the coast, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-118">76</a>; inaccurate in details of interior, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-119">77</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Permanent Settlement of Bengal, the, of<br>Lord Cornwallis (1793) and Sir John<br>Shore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>; its hasty methods,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Persia, its cis-Indus Satrapy in India,<br>annexed by Darius (500 B.C.), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-314">272</a>; its<br>extent, population, and wealth, as reported by Herodotus, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-315">273</a>; the rock<br>edicts of Asoka probably suggested by<br>those of Darius, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-83">41</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>; Malcolm's<br>mission to, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Persian influence, on Indian religion, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-253">211</a>;<br>on art, shown in Asoka's pillars, &amp;c.,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-148">106</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-151">109</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-155">113</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-161">119</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-162">120</a>; on Indian age,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-117">75</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-182">140</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-184">142</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-188">146</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-189">147</a>; on architecture, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-200">158</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-208">166</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-239">197</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-240">198</a>; translation of<br><em>Pañchatantra</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-292">250</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Peshāwar, capital of Kanishka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-331">289</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Peshwā dynasty, of Poona, the ability and<br>success of its founder and organizer of<br>the Marāthā confederacy, and his first<br>three successors, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; their extensive<br>conquests and enforced recognition at<br>Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; their relations with the<br>British power, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-504">462</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>; the<br>blow to their power and the unity of<br>the confederacy from the defeat (1761)<br>at Pānīpat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; the disputed succession<br>and the first (1775) and second Marāthā<br>(1778) Wars, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>; the disruption of<br>the confederacy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>; the flight (1802)<br>of the seventh and last Peshwā, Bājī<br>Rao II, to British protection, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>; his<br>acceptance of the Treaty of Bassein,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>; the resentment of Sindhia at<br>his surrender of Marāthā independence,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>; his reinstatement after the third<br>War, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>; his intrigues with the chiefs<br>against the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-446">404</a>; their rising<br>and defeat (1817) in the fourth War,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>; the dethronement of the<br>Peshwā and forfeiture of his dominions,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>; his survival as a pensioner<br>till 1853, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-537">495</a>; his adopted son<br>Nāna Sāhib proclaimed eighth Peshwā<br>during the Mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>; Diaries of the<br>Peshwās, 12 and <em>n.</em></p><p class="index-entry">Peterson, Professor, his detailed reports<br>on Sanskrit manuscripts, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Philosophy (excluding ethics), and its<br>literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-303">261</a>; the doctrines of<br>transmigration, as a retributive purgatory, and of final salvation, common<br>to all schools of thought, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>; the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-604" data-page="604" data-folio="562" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n603_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 562">[p. 562]</button> conventional distinction between orthodoxy and heterodoxy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>; the six<br>orthodox systems or schools, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-295">253</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>;<br>the three heterodox, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-103">61</a>; the eclectic combination of the Vedānta, Yoga,<br>and Sāṅkhya systems expounded in<br>the <em>Bhagavadgītā</em>, an episode of the<br>Mahābhārata, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Phonetics, Vedic, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-275">233</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Pietra dura</em> (Florentine), its introduction<br>under Jahāngīr and Shāhjahān, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-169">127</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pillars and columns, inscriptions on, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>; the seven famous ‘pillar-edicts’<br>of Asoka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-84">42</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>; the most notable<br>inscribed column at Allahābād, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>;<br>other noteworthy inscribed columns, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pindāris, nondescript freebooters in Central India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-536">494</a>, associated with the<br>Marāthās and unchecked till 1817 by<br>the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-536">494</a>, routed out and destroyed (1817) by Lord Hastings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-486">444</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-537">495</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Piprahwa, <em>stūpa</em> at, the oldest (450 <span class="sc">b. c.</span>)<br>Indian building, described, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-144">102</a>; the oldest record on a vase therein, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-85">43</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-97">55</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-145">103</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-175">133</a>; the contemporary stage of civilization, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-144">102</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-145">103</a>; the great gap between<br>its date and the reign (272–231 <span class="sc">b. c.</span>)<br>of Asoka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-145">103</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Plague, bubonic, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-568">526</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-569">527</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Plassey, battle of (June 23, 1757), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-517">475</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>, the date of the beginning of British<br>empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>, its immediate and local<br>results to the Company and Clive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-519">477</a>; its later and general results, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-518">476</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Poetics, or treatises on the art of poetry,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-306">264</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-307">265</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Police, reform of (1905), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-571">529</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pondicherry, founded (1674) by the<br>French, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>, its prosperity to 1706,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-505">463</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-506">464</a>; its French Governors,<br>Dumas and Dupleix (1735–54), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-512">470</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-513">471</a>; successfully defended (1748)<br>against Boscawen and Stringer Lawrence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-513">471</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-514">472</a>; invested and capitulated (1761) to Coote, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-515">473</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Ponwārs, Rājput clan (<span class="sc">a. d.</span> 831–1100)<br>of Mālwā, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-353">311</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>; their famous king<br>Bhoja, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-353">311</a>; their overthrow by the<br>Solankis, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Poona. <em>See</em> Peshwā.</p><p class="index-entry">Popham, Captain, his brilliant storm of<br>Gwalior (1780), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-527">485</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Portuguese settlements: the voyage of<br>Vasco da Gama (1498) to Calicut, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-488">446</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>, of Cabral (1500) to Calicut and<br>Cochin, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>; the Papal Bull and grant<br>to the King of Portugal (1502), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>;<br>the expedition of Albuquerque, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>;<br>the vice-royalty and policy of Almeida<br>(1505–9), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-489">447</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>; the opposition of<br>the Sultān of Egypt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>; the kindliness<br>of the Vijayanagar Rāya, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-388">346</a>; Albuquerque Viceroy (1509–15), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>, seized<br>Goa (1510), the future capital of Portuguese India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>, his political and just<br>rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; the general cruelty and<br>corruption of Portuguese rule, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-490">448</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>; the designs of Da Cunha (1529–<br>38) on Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; the siege of Diu<br>by sea and land, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>; the defeat of the<br>Turkish and Egyptian fleet and of the<br>Sultān of Gujarāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-491">449</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>; De Castro<br>(1545–8), warrior, statesman, and friend<br>of St. Francis Xavier, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>; Portuguese triumphs over the allied Muhammadans (1558–1628), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-493">451</a>; the<br>loss of maritime and commercial supremacy due to the drain on Portugal,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-493">451</a>, to the sacrifice of Portuguese to<br>Spanish interests during the union<br>(1580–1640) of the two kingdoms, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-493">451</a>,<br>and to the rise of the Dutch and English<br>powers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-493">451</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-494">452</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-497">455</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-509">467</a>; Indo-Portuguese coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-191">149</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Porus, powerful king of territory between<br>the Jhelum and the Chenāb, his resistance to Alexander, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-316">274</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-317">275</a>; his failure<br>to prevent the passage of the Jhelum,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-317">275</a>; the severe and decisive battle<br>near the town of Jhelum, 275 and <em>n.</em>;<br>his generous treatment by Alexander,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-317">275</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-318">276</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-319">277</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pottery, primitive, hand- and wheel-made,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-136">94</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>, no signs of, in palaeolithic remains, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-131">89</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>; found in neolithic tombs,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>, terra-cotta coffins and sarcophagi,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-137">95</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-138">96</a>, and seldom elsewhere, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-174">132</a>; the<br>engraved <em>lota</em> of the second century <span class="sc">b. c.</span><br>at South Kensington, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-175">133</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Prākrit, its relation to Sanskrit and Pāli,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-250">208</a>; its use in the Sanskrit drama, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-287">245</a>;<br>the language of Jainism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-250">208</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-303">261</a>, and<br>of old Buddhism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-250">208</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-301">259</a>; its secular<br>literature, folk-lore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>; its lyrics, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>;<br>its drama, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>; its <em>kāvyas</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-309">267</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-310">268</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Priests, in the older Rigvedic period, royal<br>bards and sacrificial substitutes for the<br>king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-265">223</a>, not yet an hereditary caste,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>, their specialization and supremacy<br>a growth of time, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-266">224</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Prithwī Rāj, the last Hindu (Chauhān)<br>king (1172–92) of Delhi and Ajmer,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-354">312</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>; his abduction of the princess<br>of Kanauj, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>; overthrow of the<br>Chandel king, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-356">314</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-357">315</a>; his first victories over and final defeat near Thānesar by the Muhammadans, and death,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-357">315</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-395">353</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>; his bard and chronicler,<br>Chand Bardāī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-346">304</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Prose and verse, their use in epigraphy,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-45">3</a>, in Vedic and post-Vedic literature,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-276">234</a>; in vernacular literature, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-457">415</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Prussian Companies, favoured or founded<br>by Frederick the Great (1750), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-508">466</a>;<br>the Bengal Company of Embden, its <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-605" data-page="605" data-folio="563" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n604_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 563">[p. 563]</button> ultimate failure due to the jealousy of<br>its European rivals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-508">466</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-509">467</a>, the fears<br>of the Nawāb, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-509">467</a>, and the exigencies<br>of Frederick's European diplomacy,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-509">467</a>; the complaints and refusal of the<br>East India Company to trade with it,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-509">467</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Ptolemy, geography of, a mere compilation, of general not of particular use,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-119">77</a>; instances of his misconceptions and<br>ignorance, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-120">78</a>; the primary errors in<br>the basis of his calculations, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-119">77</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-120">78</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Pulakesin II, greatly extended the Chalukya power eastward and southward<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-369">327</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>, defeated the Mauryas, reduced<br>kingdoms, and repulsed Harsha of<br>Kanauj, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-369">327</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>, his widespread<br>fame, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>; founded (A. D. 615) a new<br>Eastern Chalukya dynasty at Vengi,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-370">328</a>; his patronage of art, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-161">119</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-162">120</a>; the<br>Aihole inscription of his time, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-111">69</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Pygmy-marked’ coins, of silver, the<br>earliest, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-177">135</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>; in North India,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>, in South India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-192">150</a>; of copper,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-192">150</a>; of gold, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-194">152</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Punjab, annexation (1849) of, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>. <em>See</em><br><em>also under</em> Northern India <em>and</em> Sikhs.</p><p class="index-entry">Purāṇas, the, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-56">14</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-57">15</a>; some use made of<br>Vaṁśāvalis in their historical chapters,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-56">14</a>; instances of their meagre, inexact,<br>and self-contradictory treatment of<br>history, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-56">14</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-57">15</a>; their great omissions,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-56">14</a>; their treatment of contemporary<br>dynasties as successive, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-57">15</a>; the hopeless confusion from accepting their date<br>for Asoka, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-66">24</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Purāṇas, the, epic and didactic poems,<br>later than and partially derived from<br>the Mahābhārata, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-278">236</a>, attributable to<br>a common source, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-278">236</a>, and commonly<br>inculcating the sectarian worship of<br>Vishṇu, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-278">236</a>; individual Purāṇas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-278">236</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-279">237</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Purāṇas</em>, or silver punch-marked coins,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-178">136</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-179">137</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-193">151</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Purusha, and other cosmogonic hymns<br>of the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-260">218</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-261">219</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">‘Pygmy flints,’ immense numbers discovered in the Vindhya Hills and elsewhere, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-134">92</a>, generally, as elsewhere, in<br>the floors of dwellings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-135">93</a>, their frequently minute size and probable use,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-135">93</a>; of early Neolithic period, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-135">93</a>;<br>possibly the work of palaeolithic slaves<br>of neolithic masters, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/06-chapter-ii-prehistoric-antiquities/#page-135">93</a>.</p><h4 class="sec-h3">Q.</h4><p class="index-entry">Qabājah, lieutenant of Muḥammad<br>Ghorī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-397">355</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-400">358</a>; ruler (1206–28) of<br>Sind and Multān, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-412">370</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Qutb Shāhīs of Golconda and Hyderābād,<br>Muḥammadan dynasty (1512–1687)<br>founded by a revolter from the Bahmanīs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>; the usual wars, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>;<br>joined in the destruction of Vijayanagar,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>; foundation (1612) of Hyderābād,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>; paid tribute to the Mughals (1635),<br>to the Marāthās (1667), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>; extinguished (1687) by Aurangzeb, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>;<br>a table of the dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-432">390</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Qutb-ud-dīn, Ībak (of the Powerless<br>Finger), his conquests (1192–1203) for<br>his master, Muḥammad Ghorī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-396">354</a>,<br>Slave king of Delhi (1206–10), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-399">357</a>,<br>Iyaldūz's revolt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-400">358</a>; built the great<br>Qutbī mosque, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-399">357</a>. <em>See also under</em><br>Kutb Minār.</p><h4 class="sec-h3">R.</h4><p class="index-entry">Rafī-ud-darajāt and Rafī-ud-daulah, Mughal emperors (1719), sickly puppets<br>in the hands of the Saiyid brothers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-448">406</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Rāghavapāṇḍavīya</em>, an epic (A. D. 800) of<br>artificial ingenuity by Kavirāja, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-283">241</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Raghuba, claimant to Peshwāship, with<br>British support, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-484">442</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Railings, on Buddhist <em>stūpas</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-147">105</a>, 159–<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-203">161</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Railways system, inaugurated by Lord<br>Dalhousie, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Rājarāja I, energetic king (985–1012) of<br>the Chōlas, his wide conquests, including Ceylon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-375">333</a>; his successful interference in the Eastern Chālukyan kingdom, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-376">334</a>; several<br>wrong identifications, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-122">80</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-123">81</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sagauli, Treaty of (1815), regulating our<br>present relations with Nepāl, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-536">494</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Saiyid brothers, the two, ‘the king-makers,’ made four and overthrew two<br>Mughal emperors, <a class="index-folio-link" class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-448">406</a>, their alliance with,<br>and concession (1718) of <em>chauth</em> to, the<br>Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" discontent, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-449">407</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Saiyid dynasty of Delhi (1414–51), <a class="index-folio-link" class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sakas (or Parthians), tribes of the upper<br>Jaxartes, displaced and thrust forward<br>by the Yueh-chi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a>, overwhelmed<br>the Graeco-Bactrian kingdoms, <a and parts of Northern<br>India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" C.–A. D. 50),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>; the legend of St. Thomas and<br>king Gondophares, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-289">247</a>; the use<br>of a ring in, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sakyas (of tradition), the kinsmen of<br>Buddha, enshrined his relics, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-144">102</a>, ruthlessly massacred, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-97">55</a>, the name confused<br>with Sakas, 5 (<em>n.</em>).</p><p class="index-entry">Sālbai, Treaty of (1782), between Warren<br>Hastings and the Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" I<br>(<span class="sc">A. D.</span> 326-75), his energy, ambition,<br>and accomplishments, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-332">290</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-333">291</a>; the<br>great extent of his empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-333">291</a>; as<br>emperor of India revived the long<br>obsolete ‘horse-sacrifice,’ 38, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-333">291</a>; the<br>value and interest of the Sanskrit record<br>of his deeds, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-333">291</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-334">292</a>; his eulogy on<br>the Asoka column at Allahābād, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-92">50</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sānchī, the ruins of <em>stūpas</em> and other<br>buildings described, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-150">108</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-151">109</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-201">159</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-203">161</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sangala, the siege, capture, and razing of,<br>by Alexander, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-318">276</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-319">277</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Śaṅkara or Śaṅkarāchārya (b. <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 788),<br>the famous commentator upon the<br>Vedānta textbook, the <em>Brāhma-sūtra</em>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-296">254</a>; largely associated with the revival of Brāhmanism and the rejection<br>of Buddhism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-296">254</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Śaṅkaravarman (<span class="sc">A. D.</span> 883-902), a famous<br>warrior and builder-king of Kashmīr,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-352">310</a>; the permanence of his revenue<br>system, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-352">310</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sāṅkhya, an orthodox but godless system of philosophy, founded by Kapila<br>(c. 550 <span class="sc">B. C.</span>), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-299">257</a>; a theory of<br>evolutionary dualism opposed to earlier<br>monism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>; its cosmogony, a recurring series of cycles of differentiation by<br><em>guṇas</em>, evolution, existence, and dissolution, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-261">219</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>; its psychology of the<br>relations between matter and soul and<br>their final isolation, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-298">256</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-299">257</a>; next to<br>the Vedānta in influence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-299">257</a>; the basis<br>of Jainism and Buddhism, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sanskrit literature, ch. <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/03-introductory-notes/#page-12">vi</a>, pp. <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-248">206</a>-<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-311">269</a>;<br>its importance as the key to Hindu<br>civilization and as a guide to the study of<br>human evolution, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-248">206</a>;
