attacked by Ibrāhīm, Lodī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-409">367</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Seven Pagodas. <em>See</em> Māmallapuram.</p><p class="index-entry">Sewell, Mr., his <em>Indian Calendar</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-107">65</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Shāh Ālam, Mughal emperor (1759–<br>1806), son and successor of Ālamgīr II,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>; a fugitive from his father's<br>Wazīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-452">410</a>; his attempts to conquer<br>Bihār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-520">478</a>; after the battle of<br>Buxar resided (1764–71) at Allāhābād<br>under British protection, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-521">479</a>; the<br>crushing defeat of the Marāthās by<br>the Muhammadans under Ahmad Shāh,<br>Durrānī (1761) at Pānīpat, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, their<br>reappearance (1769) and invitation<br>(1771) of Shāh Ālam to Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>;<br>his reign at Delhi (1771–1803) generally under Marāthā guard and control,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>; seized and blinded (1788), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>;<br>Lord Lake's defeat (1803) of the Marāthās and British occupation of Delhi,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>; British coinage in his name, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-190">148</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Shāhjahān, fifth Mughal emperor of India<br>(1627–58), eldest son and successor of<br>Jahāngīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-442">400</a>; his operations in the<br>Deccan, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-442">400</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; Kandahār ceded<br>(1637) by the Persians, held for eleven<br>years, retaken and lost thenceforth to<br>the Mughal empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; his illness<br>and the struggle among his sons for the<br>throne, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; his deposition (1658) by<br>the victor, Aurangzeb, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; the magnificence of his court, the glory of his<br>empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; the Tāj Mahal and his<br>other buildings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-443">401</a>; his patronage of<br>Florentine artists, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/07-chapter-iii-archaeology-of-historical-period/#page-169">127</a>; his coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-189">147</a>; the most magnificent of Indian<br>builders, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-242">200</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Shāhu, grandson and heir of Sivājī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>;<br>his long nominal reign (1707–48), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>;<br>the transfer of power to his minister,<br>the Peshwā, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-483">441</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sharkī dynasty and architecture. <em>See</em><br>Jaunpur.</p><p class="index-entry">Sher Shāh, Sūr, Afghan emperor of Delhi<br>(1540–45), one of the greatest rulers of<br>India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a>; proclaimed himself (1539)<br>king of Bengal and Bihār, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-315">273</a>; expelled<br>Humāyūn from India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a>; annexed<br>Mālwā and took Chitor, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-437">395</a>; his extensive and permanent reforms of the<br>coinage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-187">145</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-188">146</a>; mausoleum of, at<br>Sasarām, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-225">183</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Shore, Sir John (Lord Teignmouth),<br>Governor-General (1793–8), responsible<br>for the details of Cornwallis's Permanent Settlement of Bengal, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-528">486</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-529">487</a>;<br>his refusal to help the Nizām (1795)<br>against the Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-530">488</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Shujā-ud-daulah, Nawāb of Oudh, reluctantly received Shāh Ālam, a fugitive<br>from Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>; lost the battle of<br>Buxar (1764) to the British, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>; conquered and annexed Rohilkhand (1774)<br>with British aid, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-453">411</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-454">412</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Siddharājā, famous Rājput king and magician (1093–1143) of Gujarāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-355">313</a>;<br>carried about the Ponwār king in a<br>cage, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-355">313</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Signet-rings of rulers and their ministers,<br>or their impressed stamp on wax or<br>clay or cloth, or their reproductions in<br>metal, described, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-72">30</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-75">33</a>; as vouchers<br>and means of identification, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-72">30</a>; as<br>seals to copper grants, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-71">29</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-74">32</a>; the<br>signet-ring of the Mahārāja Mahéśvaranāga, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-68">26</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-73">31</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sikandar, the Idol-Breaker, king (1386–<br>1410) of Kashmīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-415">373</a>; permanently<br>turned his subjects to Muhammadanism,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-415">373</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sikandar, Lodī, king of Delhi (1489–<br>1517), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-409">367</a>; popular, able, and a patron<br>of learning, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-409">367</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sikandar, Sūr, claimant and temporary<br>holder of the Sūr throne of Delhi, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-438">396</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sikh Wars, the first (1845) provoked by<br>the Sikhs, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; the four pitched battles<br>of Mūdkī, Firozshāh, Aliwāl, and Sobraon, fought by Sir Hugh Gough, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>;<br>the moderate terms of peace imposed<br>by Sir Henry Hardinge in the Treaty<br>of Lahore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; the second (1848–9),<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>; the losses of the British at<br>Chiliānwāla, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>; capture of Multān,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>; Gough's crowning victory at<br>Gujrāt, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Sikhara</em>, or pyramidal spire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-215">173</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-217">175</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-222">180</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-610"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="610" data-folio="568" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n609_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 568">[p. 568]</button></div><p class="index-entry">Sikhs, the, a religious Hindu sect under<br>military discipline, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-544">502</a>; their origin,<br>history, and sufferings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-544">502</a>; with the<br>Marāthās co-heirs of the Mughal empire, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-544">502</a>; their confederacies along<br>the Sutlej, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-544">502</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; welded into one<br>kingdom with capital at Lahore by<br>Ranjīt Singh (1780-1839), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; his<br>engagement (1809) to Metcalfe not to<br>cross the Sutlej, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; the dissensions<br>after his death, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; the first Sikh<br>War (1845) and the moderate terms of<br>peace, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>; Major Henry Lawrence<br>appointed Resident at Lahore, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-545">503</a>;<br>second Sikh War (1848-9), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-546">504</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-547">505</a>;<br>their loyalty during the Mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-553">511</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">of pure theists, quietists in religion,<br>alchemists in science, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>; a version of<br>one of their mystical poems, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-478">436</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Śiva, a minor god in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-255">213</a>, as<br>Rudra terrible, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-255">213</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-256">214</a>; in the later<br>Vedas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>; in post-Vedic literature,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-275">233</a>; the rise of the Hindu cult, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-347">305</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-348">306</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-359">317</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-365">323</a>; his cult and literature, 425-<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>; in the Tamiḷ country and Kanara,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-467">425</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-468">426</a>; in Bengal for long replaced<br>by the cult of his bride, Durgā, his<br>Energic Principle, under the name of<br>Kālī or Chaṇḍī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-468">426</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>; the one god<br>of the Śittars, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-477">435</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Sivājī Bhonsla (1627-80), the national<br>hero of the Marāthās, and founder of<br>their power, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-481">439</a>, founded a kingdom<br>and created a nation, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>; his rising<br>against the Muhammadan powers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-444">402</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>; sacked Surat but had to surrender<br>to Aurangzeb, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>; re-established his<br>power and was crowned at Raigarh,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-445">403</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-482">440</a>; Mysore War (1799)<br>and storming of Seringapatam, <a class="index-folio-link" class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-532">490</a>; the<br>brilliant victories of the third Marāthā<br>War (1802-4), and its acquisitions, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>;<br>the repulse (1805) of Lake at Bharatpur<br>and other disasters, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>; the success, completeness, and financial expense<br>of his aggressive policy, North and<br>South, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-534">492</a>; the three 'politicals' of his<br>school, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-535">493</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Wellesley, General A. (afterwards Duke<br>of Wellington), his victories of Assaye<br>and Argaum over the Marāthās, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-485">443</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-533">491</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Western Hindī, vernacular poets of, Chand<br>Bardāī and the bardic chronicles, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-469">427</a>,<br><a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/15-chapter-xi-vernacular-literature/#page-470">428</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-615"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="615" data-folio="573" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n614_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 573">[p. 573]</button></div><p class="index-entry">White Huns, the, of Khwārizm, their invasion (A. D. 430) of the Roman empire under Theodosius repelled, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a>; their overthrow (480) of the Gupta empire and all order, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-182">140</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-183">141</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a>; the cruel tyranny (528) in Kashmīr of their chief, Mihirakula, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-336">294</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Willoughby, Sir Hugh, his failure (1533) to force the North-East passage, and death, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-495">453</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Wilson, Professor H. H., his <em>Descriptive Catalogue of the Mackenzie Collection</em>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>; mention therein of fanciful, inaccurate, early chronicles, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-49">7</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Wilson, Mr. James, financial member of Council, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-557">515</a>; his financial reforms after the Mutiny, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-558">516</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Wives, the position of, in the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-267">225</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Wooden architecture, early, of India, China, Japan, and Burma, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>; appropriate to a tropical climate, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>; its gradual transition to stone, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-199">157</a>; its former existence inferred from the style of early Buddhist <em>stūpas</em> and their railings, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-198">156</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-201">159</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-202">160</a>, from the construction of cave temples, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-204">162</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-205">163</a>, and of Jain and Dravidian temples, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-212">170</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/09-chapter-v-architecture/#page-214">172</a>; the transition to stone part of the revived (Rājput) civilization, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-357">315</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/12-chapter-viii-mediaeval-history-of-northern-india-the-hindu-pe/#page-358">316</a>.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">X.</h3><p class="index-entry">Xavier, St. Francis, missionary pioneer, his intimacy with De Castro, the Portuguese Viceroy, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/17-chapter-xiii-the-early-european-settlements/#page-492">450</a>.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">Y.</h3><p class="index-entry">Yādavas of Deogiri, the, assumed independence (1187) after the fall of the Chālukyas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-382">340</a>; their successful struggle for the Deccan with the Hoysalas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-383">341</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-384">342</a>; Alā-ud-dīn's invasion (1294), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-384">342</a>; the pusillanimity of king Rāmachandra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-384">342</a>; the courage of his son and successor, Samkara, and refusal to pay tribute, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-384">342</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-385">343</a>; his defeat, capture (1312), and death, by Malik Kāfūr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-385">343</a>; the end of the dynasty, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/13-chapter-ix-the-hindu-period-of-southern-india/#page-385">343</a>; the historical pedigree of, by Hemādri, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-62">20</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-63">21</a>; its famous descendant, Sivājī, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/16-chapter-xii-the-marathas/#page-481">439</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Yājñavalkya, code of (c. A. D. 350), <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-304">262</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Yajurveda, the, one of the later Vedas, largely borrowed from the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-269">227</a>–<a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>; its more easterly locality, Kurukshetra, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-269">227</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>; its six recensions of the two Black and two White schools, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>; the greater prominence of sacrifice and of caste, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-270">228</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-271">229</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Yakūb Khān, Treaty of Gandamak with (1879), subsequently deported to India, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/18-chapter-xiv-history-of-british-rule/#page-560">518</a>.</p><p class="index-entry"><em>Yaśastilaka</em>, the, of Sōmadēva (252, 267), the date of its completion (A. D. 959) given in the colophon, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>; his vague reference to his unnamed patron, the feudatory of a Kṛishṇa, supplemented and confirmed by literary and inscriptional evidence, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-61">19</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/05-chapter-i-epigraphy/#page-62">20</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Yoga, an orthodox system of philosophy, of the second century B. C., based on the dualism of the Sāṅkhya, but illogically thrusting in the idea of a personal God, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-299">257</a>; the freedom of the soul from matter attained by mental asceticism or ecstatic abstraction, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>; the modern Yogīs conjurers and jugglers, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-300">258</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Yueh-chi, a nomad horde in the steppes of Asia, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a>; its migration (165 B. C.) westward and pressure upon the Sakas, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-329">287</a>; their further advance through Bactria and across Hindu Kush, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>; their union under the Kushān dynasty, their dominion both sides of the Oxus, and annexation of Afghānistān, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>; displaced Hermaeus, last Greek prince of Kābul, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/11-chapter-vii-early-history-of-northern-india-from-600-b-c-to-a/#page-330">288</a>. <em>See also under</em> Kushān.</p><h3 class="sec-h2">Z.</h3><p class="index-entry">Zain-ul-ābidīn, just and tolerant king (1417) of Kashmīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-415">373</a>; built mosques and made canals, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/14-chapter-x-muhammadan-india-a-d-637-1803/#page-416">374</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Zodiacal coins, of Jahāngīr, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/08-chapter-iv-numismatics/#page-189">147</a>.</p><p class="index-entry">Zoology of the Rigveda, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-258">216</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-259">217</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-262">220</a>, <a class="index-folio-link" href="/books/imperial-gazetteer-of-india-vol-02-historical/chapters/10-chapter-vi-sanskrit-literature/#page-263">221</a>.</p><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-616"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="616" data-folio="574" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/imperialgazette02hunt/page/n615_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 574">[p. 574]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="imprint-line">OXFORD
